                     tamiḻ paḻamoḻikaḷ tokuppum
               āṅkila moḻipeyarppum, āciriyar percivāl
                    Tamil Proverbs with Their Translation
                             by Rev. P. Percival

   Source:
   Tamil Proverbs with their English Translation
   containing upwards of Six thousand proverbs
   By The Rev. P. Percival,
   Chaplain, Madras Military Female Orphan Asylum:
   Author of the "Land of the Veda" etc.
   Second Edition, 1874
   Madras: Printed and Published at the Dinavartamani Press,
   Little Bourne, Mylapore
   --------------
   PREFACE TO THE FIRST EDITION.
   It is hoped that the following collection of Tamil Proverbs may be
   useful to those who are in any way connected with the interesting
   people who speak that language. Examples of concise and forcible
   expression are thereby furnished, which may tend to aid in the study of
   the language, and occasional allusions to national, social, and
   religious usages may suggest heads of valuable enquiry, while the modes
   of thinking, and the natural shrewdness of the Hindu mind may, be seen
   through this medium. It is presumed that the translation generally
   conveys the sense of the original, although not always with its force,
   nor with theelegance that might be desired. Should it be called for, a
   future edition may remedy some of the existing faults,and also supply
   deficiencies, both as to the character of the translation, and the
   number of the examples.
   P. PERCIVAL.
   JAFFNA, Christmas, 1842.
   ---------------
   PREFACE TO THE SECOND EDITION.
   A Few words on the history of the book now offered to the Public in an
   enlarged form may not be out of place in the Preface to the Second
   Edition.
   Almost immediately after my arrival in this country in 1826, I entered
   on my Missionary work among the Tamil people, having acquired the
   rudiments of their language in England from a gentleman who had spent
   several years in the South of India and North Ceylon. Intercourse with
   the natives afforded me ample opportunities of becoming acquainted with
   their modes of thought and expression, and, noticing their predilection
   for proverbial sayings, I was led to an early study and use of Tamil
   proverbs.
   In 1830 I was removed to Bengal. There I became acquainted with a
   clergyman who had, like myself, turned his attention to the proverbs of
   the people among whom he dwelt. He collected a considerable number of
   Bengali proverbs, and published them, with an English translation, in a
   small volume, printed at the Press connected with Bishop's College,
   Calcutta. When the Mission in Bengal with which I was connected was
   withdrawn, I returned to my former sphere of labor among the Tamil
   people. As opportunity offered, I collected and arranged proverbs that
   I found current among them; and in 1842 printed a collection of nearly
   nineteen hundred with an English translation.
   PREFACE.
   The First Edition having become scarce, I was urged, by many whose
   judgment I felt bound to respect, to prepare matter for a second and
   enlarged edition. Various causes prevented me from immediately
   undertaking the work. For several years all my time was devoted to the
   revision of the Tamil Scriptures, in conjunction with Messrs. RHENIUS,
   KNIGHT, SPAULDING, HUTCHINGS, and others, and in passing the “Tentative
   Bible” through the Press. Subsequently a visit to England caused a
   further postponement of my plan, but, on my return to Madras in 1854,
   my attention was again directed to the subject of Tamil proverbs.
   In the following year a Committee of gentlemen belonging to the Civil
   and Military Services of Government, Messrs. STOKES, SIM, CHAMIER, and
   Colonels BROWN, PEARS, and BELL resolved to start a Journal, for the
   purpose of diffusing, among the Tamil people, information social,
   political, and literary. The Committee invited my co-operation as
   Editor. This labor I at once accepted, the more readily because for
   several months I had been making arrangements to carry out the same
   idea, and had visited Calcutta to procure information on the subject,
   chiefly from Mr. MARSHMAN. In October 1855 the first number of the new
   journal, the Dinavartamáni, was published. Soon after this I also
   undertook the editing of a Telugu journal of the same scope, and
   bearing the same name. The circulation of these papers throughout the
   Madras Presidency afforded me a good opportunity of inviting the
   subscribers to assist me in the collection of Tamil and Telugu
   proverbs. My request was met by contributions from all parts of the
   country. The former were added to a rapidly increasing collection,
   while a selection from the latter was published, from week to week,
   along with an
   English translation, in the Telugu Dinavartamâni. When the late Major
   CARR informed me that he was preparing a volume of Telugu proverbs for
   the Press, I made over to him my collection, a service he thus
   acknowledged in the Preface to his work “The Translator hns to thank
   the Rev. P. PERCIVAL, Professor of Vernacular Literature, Presidency
   College, for kindly placing at his disposal a MS. containing a large
   number of Telugu proverbs.”
   In the year 1860 my collection of Tamil proverbs contained nearly five
   thousand examples translated into English and ready for the Press. At
   that time, however, I was so much engaged in preparing books and maps,
   in the Vernaculars, for the Director of Public Instruction, that I
   found it impossible to carry out my design of printing these proverbs,
   although I had announced it in the Dinavartamâni. Then followed an
   interval of ten years during which I was engaged in duties absorbing
   all my time and attention. I had the superintendence of the Public
   Instruction Press on my hands; and, aided by the Pundits of the
   Presidency College, and other competent native scholars, had
   theresponsibility of editing, among the works that issued from the
   Press during this period, seven dictionaries, some of which have since
   been re-printed.
   At the beginning of 1870, having been relieved from my heavy duties in
   connexion with the Presidency College and the University, I resolved to
   print the proverbs as they stood, and soon. after this having met the
   Rev. G. FRYAR of Manargudi, Tanjore District, I learnt that he had for
   eight or nine years been also engaged in collecting Tamil proverbs, and
   that his collection then amounted to five thousand examples, and that
   he proposed eventually to publish it on the basis of my First Edition.
   On
   hearing of my own plan, Mr. FRYAR abandoned this purpose, and very
   generously placed his valuable MS. at my disposal, from which I
   obtained several hundred new proverbs.
   Shortly after this, again, one of my compositors brought me an
   old-looking MS. containing over fifteen hundred examples. A volume
   printed in 1861 at Vepery, and containing nearly four thousand
   examples, was also put into my hands. But these collections yielded
   very few proverbs not already contained in my own MS. A Pundit of the
   Government Normal School furnished me with about a hundred fresh
   examples, and a small MS. belonging to a Native Christian lady of
   Tanjore contained a few curious proverbs that I had not seen before.
   These related to Christian character, and evidently originated in
   anything but a benign feeling.
   Altogether my collections in 1872 exceeded fifteen thousand, but they
   only yielded the number contained in this volume. That the outcome of
   so large a gathering should be comparatively small may easily be
   accounted for by the fact that the several collections, in very many
   instances, contained the same proverbs. It must be borne in mind that
   my own collection, which eventually absorbed the others, was made
   during a period extending over forty years, and that, through the
   medium of the Dinavartamâni, I had received contributions from all
   parts of the Madras Presidency, as well from North Ceylon. Hence it was
   but natural that I should anticipate collectors who come into the field
   later.
   But for the omission of many of the admired Aphorisms of the ancient
   Tamil matron, Avveyar, this collection would have been somewhat larger,
   Though these Aphorisms are compositions of
   unequalled brevity and beauty, I have, for the most part, omitted them,
   because they are generally taught in schools, and indeed regarded as
   part of a school course. En passant I may mention that seven or eight
   years ago I printed and published them, in a separate form, with an
   analysis and translation in English, and that a Second Edition of this
   work is now in the Press. Bible proverbs, and those obviously Sanskrit,
   are also omitted, because this collection is designed to be Dravidian.
   Translations from Western proverbs I have for the same reason left out.
   In this edition, as in the First, the proverbs are arranged in
   alphabetical order. This of course is the easiest method. Some years
   ago I thought of classifying the subjects somewhat after the plan of
   Amarakosha, a Sanskrit Dictionary, wherein objects are divided into
   separate classes, as Supernals, Infernals, Men, Animals, &c. A division
   of subjects on the principle of a commonplace book was also thought of,
   and a friend whose judgment I greatly respect, suggested an index.
   Although each of the methods referred to has much to recommend it, at
   length I gave them all up, and resolved to adopt the easiest-the
   alphabetical arrangement. Had I decided otherwise, the work must have
   been delayed ; andas in my case “ the day is far spent,” I might not
   have been able to accomplish my purpose.
   On the analogy of the proverbial expressions current in all countries,
   many scraps of sentences, mere allusions, similitudes, and the like,
   are included in this collection, though not proverbs in the proper
   sense of the term. The objects of Nature, habits, and dispositions of
   animals, government, agriculture and commerce, domestic and religious
   usages supply a large number of proverbial expressions. The incidents
   of classical literature contribute many that prove highly acceptable to
   those who have
   'any acquaintance with the sources of Hindu history. So largely do the
   stories of ancient writers contribute that the fact hasoriginated a
   proverb universal among the people of Bengal.
   What is there after Bhárata? A glance at a few pages of this volume
   will suffice to show that the people among whom such proverbs are
   current cannot be ignorant of the laws of God.
   Vox Populi, vox Dei, may be applied to this aspect of many of the
   Dravidian proverbs :
   “ The people's voice, the voice of God we call ; And what are Proverbs,
   but the people's voice ? Coined first and common made, by common choice
   ;
   Then sure they must have weight, and truth withal." I may be permitted
   to say a few words on the utility of a volume like that now offered to
   the public. A foreigner destined to spend the best part of his life
   among the Tamil people will find their proverbs of inestimable value.
   In these pithy and instructive sayings a vast fund of information will
   be found, not to be obtained from ordinary books, and not at all from
   books designed for Western minds ; —practical maxims, resulting from
   the experience of many generations, maxims that will be sought in vain
   in books of modern origin.
   The translation will, I hope, be generally approved. In some instances
   I have found it difficult to ascertain the meaning ; and in many the
   application has equally puzzled both myself and others to whom I have
   applied for information. In some cases I may have been misled by those
   who might be more disposed to hazard a conjecture than to acknowledge
   ignorance. For the benefit of foreigners the occasional notes might
   have been
   advantageously increased in number, but usages differ so much that
   great caution is required in this matter. So varied is the operation of
   race, language, religion, and climate, upon the character and social
   usages of the Hindus who occupy the vast area of India that it is
   unsafe to assume generally that what is common in one Province is so in
   others,
   In conclusion, any one interested in this work will oblige by sending
   me proverbs not found in the collection, or any suggestions for
   improving the next Edition. Though I may not hope to see another
   edition, my son-in-law, Mr. W. A. SYMONDS, who has long studied Tamil
   proverbs with great interest, will, should it be called for, undertake
   the editing.
   LITTLE BOURNE, MADRAS,
           P. PERCIVAL, February 13, 1874.
                     ___________________________________

             TAMIL PROVERBS, WITH THEIR TRANSLATION IN ENGLISH.
        tamiḻ paḻamoḻikaḷ tokuppum āṅkila moḻipeyarppum
                        Part 1 (first 3000 proverbs)

   1. akaṅkaiyiṟ pōṭṭup puṟaṅkaiyai nakkalāmā?
   Having placed the thing on the palm, why lick the back of the hand?
   2. akaṭavikaṭamāyp pēcukiṟāṉ.
   He speaks artfully.
   3. akatikku ākāyam tuṇai.
   Heaven is the help of the helpless.
   4. akatikkut teyvamē tuṇai.
   God Himself is the help of the helpless.
   5. akati col ampalam ēṟātu.
   The word of the destitute does not reach the assembly.
   An assembly of learned men or men in power. The words of the poor,
   whether they relate to oppression, or to other injuries, or to opinion,
   are not likely to find admission where alone they can avail.
   6. akati talaiyiṟ poḻutu viṭikiṟatu.
   Light breaks on the head of the destitute.
   Blame, or suspicion, will fall on the head of the unprotected and
   friendless. The poor are at work by break of day.
   7. akati peṟuvatu peṇpiḷḷai, atuvum veḷḷi pūrāṭam.
   The destitute brings forth a female child, and that on Friday, under
   the star Purádam.
   Used of one suffering from an accumulation of evils. The condition of
   the parent, the sex of the child, the day of its birth and its ruling
   star are alike inauspicious.
   8. akatiyaip pakuti kēṭkiṟatā?
   What! is tribute demanded of the destitute ?
   9. akattil aḻaku mukattil teriyum.
   The beauty of the mind appears in the face.
   10. akatti āyiram kāy kāyttālum puṟatti puṟattiyē.
   Though the Agatti-Coronilla grandiflora.—brings forth by thousands, its
   fruit remains ungathered.
   Spoken of the beneficent acts of one not held in estimation, and whose
   kindness is not therefore appreciated. The proverb is also used of a
   miser whose treasures are useless.
   11. akappaṭṭukkoḷvēṉ eṉṟō kaḷḷaṉ kaḷaveṭukkiṟatu ?
   Does the thief steal in expectation of being caught ?
   12. akappaṭṭavaṉukku aṣṭamattuc caṉi, ōṭippōṉavaṉukku oṉpatām iṭattu
   rājā.
   He who was caught was under the influence of Saturn in the eighth sign,
   and he who escaped, did so, under the influence of Saturn in the ninth.
   Saturn situated in the eighth sign from that of one's birth is supposed
   to exercise a most malignant influence. This opinion of the Hindus
   appears in many of their Proverbs.
   Hindu Astronomy is made the foundation of a vast system of Astrology.
   The real movements and the relative positions of the planets are
   wrought into a systematic connection with a great variety of arbitrary
   divisions of the signs of the Zodiac, and of the twenty-seven Lunar
   Mansions. To these bodies are added various Mythological appendages, as
   beasts, birds, trees, &c., all of which, of course, being a part of the
   fine-spun theory, help to form in the view of the people generally, a
   more recondite and imposing system than that of Astronomy itself.
   The Astrological dogmas of India have an important bearing on all
   thedomestic arrangements and practices of the people. They extend to a
   great variety of popular superstitions which run out into every
   department of life. This is seen in the lucky and unlucky months, days
   and other divisions of time, in the horoscope, which has a powerful
   controlling influence in marriage, and in other matters relating to the
   settlement of families and in the more general prognostics for the year
   as given in the Hindu Calendar. Astrology in its popular developments
   exerts a powerful influence on all classes of Hindu Society.
   13. akappai piṭittavaṉ taṉṉavaṉāṉāl, aṭippantiyil iruntā leṉṉa,
   kaṭaippantiyil iruntāleṉṉa?
   If he who has the ladle be one's own servant, what matters it whether
   one be seated first or last at the feast?
   The Agappai—ladle—here referred to is made of a piece of cocoanut shell
   with a suitable wooden handle. The shell being very hard and not porous
   is well suited for the purposes of a spoon or ladle. It is easily
   cleaned, and well adapted for earthen cooking Vessels. It may be seen
   in every cooking room or shed throughout
   the Tamil country.
   14. akam ēṟac cukam ēṟum.
   As grain becomes cheaper, enjoyment increases.
   15. akam malintāl añcum maliyum, akam kuṟaintāl añcum kuṟaiyum.
   If grain abound, the five also will abound; if grain be scarce, the
   five will be so.
   16. akam malintāl ellām maliyum, akam kuṟaintāl ellām
   kuṟaiyum.
   If grain abounds, all things abound, if grain be scarce, all things are
   scarce.
   17. akala iruntāl nīḷa uṟavu, kiṭṭa iruntāl muṭṭap pakai.
   If apart, long friendship, if together, the least touch will provoke
   hatred.
   18. akala iruntālp pakaiyum uṟavām.
   When apart, even enmity becomes friendship.
   19. akala iruntālp pukala uṟavu.
   Separation secures manifest friendship.
   20. akalvaṭṭam pakal maḻai.
   A large halo — a rainy day.
   21. akavilai aṟiyātavaṉ tukkam aṟiyāṉ.
   He who knows not the price of grain, knows not sorrow.
   22. akaḻilē viḻunta mutalaikku atuvē vaikuṇṭam.
   The moat into which the alligator has plunged is to itself Vaikundam.
   (Paradise.)
   23. akā nākkāyp pēcukiṟāṉ.
   He speaks glibly.
   24. akirttiyam ceykiṟavaṉ mukattil viḻikkiṟatā?
   Is it to see the face of one who acts improperly?
   25. akōra tapaci viparīta cōraṉ.
   A terrible ascetic, an atrocious cheat.
   26. akōra tapaci, viparīta nipuṇaṉ.
   In austerity, severe ; in perversity, an adept. Prone to extremes.
   27. akkarai māṭṭukku ikkarai paccai.
   Green pasture on this shore for the cattle on that.
   28. akkāṭu veṭṭip parutti vitaikkiṟēṉ eṉṟāl, appā eṉakkoru tuppaṭṭi
   eṉkiṟāṉ piḷḷai.
   If I say that I am about to clear away the jungle to sow cotton seed,
   my child exclaims, O father, give me a cloth. Sanguine and very
   anticipatory.
   29. akkāṭu veṭṭip parutti vitaittāl, appā muḻacciṟṟāṭai eṉkiṟatām peṇ.
   If I clear away the jungle and sow cotton seed, it is said that the
   girl exclaimed, O father, a cubit of cloth.
   30. akkāḷ irukkiṟavaraiyil maccāṉ uṟavu.
   The friendship of a brother-in-law lasts while one's sister lives.
   31. akkāḷ uṇṭāṉāl, maccāṉ uṇṭu.
   If one has a sister, he may have a brother-in-law.
   32. akkāḷ uṭaimai arici, taṅkaicci uṭaimai taviṭā?
   Is the property of the elder sister rice, and that of the younger bran?
   Said of one who is extremely chary about his own property, and
   indifferent about that of another.
   33. akkāḷ uṟavum maccāṉ pakaiyumā ?
   Whilst cherishing friendship for the sister, are you at enmity with the
   brother-in-law ?
   34. akkāḷaik koṇṭāl, taṅkaiyai muṟai kēṭpāṉēṉ?
   Having taken in marriage the elder sister, why inquire after the rank
   of the younger ?
   35. akkāḷaip paḻittut taṅkai apacāriyāṉāḷ.
   Having reproached her elder sister, the younger played the harlot.
   36. akkāḷ tāṉ kūṭappiṟantāḷ , maccāṉum kūṭap piṟantāṉā?
   My sister was indeed born of the same mother : was my brother in-law
   also ?
   37. akkirārattil piṟantālum, nāy vētam aṟiyumā?
   Will a dog understand the Vedas, although born in a Brahman village ?
   38. akkirārattukku oru āṭu vantāl, āḷukku oru mayir.
   Should a sheep come into a Brahman village, each person will get but a
   hair.
   After a sheep has been sacrificed in a Brahmanical village and the
   carcass consumed, the Brahmans may use the hair when performing poja :
   this interpretation is disputed.
   39. akkirārattu nāy piratiṣṭaikku virumpiṉatupōla.
   As a dog longed for the consecrated things of a Brahman village.
   Said when great expectations are entertained of something that is
   likely to prove illusory : an amusing story is told about this proverb.
   40. akkiṉi malaimēl karppūrapāṇam pirayōkittatu pōla.
   As one discharged a camphor arrow at a burning mountain.
   Spoken of an act done to injure another, fruitless or inadequate, and
   possibly destructive of the means employed.
   41. akkiṉi tēvaṉukku apiṣēkam ceytatu pōl irukkiṟāṉ.
   He is as if a libation had been poured out to the god of fire.
   Used of one naturally very black, who on hearing the proverb if
   heunderstands its purport, is of course very angry. When water is
   poured on burning charcoal the cinders appear exceedingly black. The
   intended reflection is not the less insulting because of the
   complimentary way in which it is conveyed.
   42. akkiṉiyāṟ cuṭṭa puṇ viṣamikkātu.
   A cauterized wound will not fester much.
   43. akkuttokku illātavaṉukkut tukkam eṉṉa?
   Whence sorrow to him who has no connections ?
   Said of one who is so obscure and isolated as to have no one whose
   griefs he can share or assuage.
   44. aṅkattai āṟṟil alaicoṇātā?
   Is it improper to bathe in a river ?
   45. aṅkattaik koṇṭupōy āṟṟil alaiciṉālum, tōṣam illai.
   Although he take his body and wash it in the river, no fault will be
   found.
   46. aṅkattaik koṉṟu āṟṟil cērkkavoṇṇātu.
   A body that has been deprived of life (murdered) may not be put into a
   river.
   47. aṅkāṅku vaipōkamāyirukkiṟāṉ, iṅkē pārttāl araikkācu mutalum illai.
   He is seen everywhere enjoying himself: when looked at in his
   homestead, he is not worth half a cash.
   Said of one who, when out, affects to be well off, whereas he has
   nothing at all to depend upon. The word (kācu) Cash, is used for
   several kinds of coin, also for
   money.
   48. aṅkāṭi vilaiyai atira aṭikkātē.
   Do not beat down the market price.
   Do not contravene the established opinions and practices of the people
   with whom you are associated.
   49. aṅkāṭikkāriyaic caṅkītam pāṭac coṉṉāl, veṅkāyam karu vēppilai
   eṉpāḷ.
   If a song be demanded of a woman going along with her market basket,
   she will exclaim venkaiyam, karuveppilai, (Onions, curry leaves.)
   50. aṅkiṭutoṭuppikku aṅkiraṇṭu kuṭṭu, iṅkiraṇṭu coṭṭu.
   One who frequently changes his party will receive two slaps here and
   three cuffs there.
   51. aṅkum tappi iṅkum tappi akappaṭṭukkoṇṭāṉ tummaṭṭippaṭṭaṉ.
   Tummattippattan, who had escaped here and there, was caught.
   Referring to the adventures of a sorcerer.
   52. aṅkum iruppāṉ iṅkum iruppāṉ, ākkiṉa cōṟṟukkup paṅkum iruppāṉ.
   He is here and there and has a share also in the boiled rice.
   53. aṅkēṇṭi makaḷē kañcikku aḻukiṟāy? iṅkē vantāl, kāṟṟāyp paṟakkalām.
   Why, my daughter, are you crying there for kanji ? come hither and you
   may fly as the wind.
   Spoken of a proffered change which may be for the worse.
   54. acal vāḻntāl, aintu nāḷ paṭṭiṉi kiṭappāḷ.
   If her neighbour prospers, she will starve herself for five days.
   55. acal vīṭu vāḻntāl paratēcam pōkiṟatā?
   Is one to go to a foreign country, because his neighbour prospers ?
   56. acalum picalum aṟiyāmal aṭuttavaṉaik keṭukkap pārkkiṟāṉ.
   Regardless of circumstances, he aims to destroy his neighbour.
   57. acavāp payirum kaṇṭatē uṟavum.
   Stunted grain—friendship at sight.
   Both valueless.
   58.acaintu tiṉkiṟatu māṭu, acaiyāmal tiṉkiṟatu vīṭu.
   A cow eats moving; a house eats standing.
   59. accam illātavaṉ ampalam ēṟuvāṉ.
   The fearless goes into the assembly.
   Used of those who have more courage than discretion.
   60. accāṇi illāta tēr muccāṇum ōṭātu.
   A car without a linch-pin will not move even three spans.
   Intimating that appliances however trivial cannot be dispensed with,
   and that the best instruments and weapons require due management.
   61. accikkup pōṉālum, akappai araikkācu.
   Though taken to Achchi, a wooden ladle will fetch only half a cash.
   Achchi being a prosperous place, cooking utensils are there is great
   demand, yet even there this inferior article will bring no more than
   its value. Spoken of the intrinsic value of a thing.
   62, acciyilum piccaikkārar uṇṭu.
   There are beggars even in Achchi.
   See the preceding proverb.
   63. añcaṉakkāraṉ mutukil vañcaṉaikkāraṉ ēṟiṉāṉ.
   The cheat has got on the shoulders of the conjurer.
   Said when one deceiver has got the better of another.
   64. añcalivantaṉam ārukkum naṉmai.
   Deferential respect is agreeable to every one.
   65. añcā neñcu paṭaittāl ārukku āvāṉ?
   Who will tolerate a presumptuous or impudent man?
   66. añcāvatu peṇ keñciṉālum kiṭaiyātu.
   A fifth born female cannot be obtained though earnestly sought.
   A fifth born female is regarded as the special favourite of fortune; an
   eighth — as the very opposite.
   67. añcilē vaḷaiyātatu aimpatilē vaḷaiyumā?
   Will that which was not bent at five, bend at fifty ?
   68. añciṉavaṉaik kuñcum veruṭṭum.
   Even a fledgeling may scare the timid.
   69. añcil aṟiyātavaṉ aimpatil aṟivāṉā?
   Will one ignorant at five understand at fifty ?
   Early instruction essential to future knowledge. The proverb may also
   suggest that growth in years will not make one, that is naturally dull,
   bright.
   70. añciṉavaṉaip pēy aṭikkum.
   Demons strike the timid.
   71. añci āṇmai cey.
   Act manfully, but modestly.
   72. añciṉāraik keñcuvikkum, aṭittārai vāḻvikkum.
   Those who were once feared may be made to beg; and they who were once
   oppressed may be made prosperous.
   73. añciṉavaṉ kaṇṇukku ākāyamellām pēy.
   To the timid the sky is full of demons.
   74. añcu kācukkuk kutiraiyum vēṇṭum, atuvum āṟṟaik kaṭakkap pāyavum
   vēṇṭum.
   A horse is required for five cash, and he must be able to leap over a
   river.
   Used in derision when great results are expected from inadequate means.
   75. añcu kuñcum kaṟiyāmō? aṟiyāp peṇṇum peṇṭāmō?
   Are five young birds a curry? Is a young girl a wife?
   76. añcukku iraṇṭu paḻutillai.
   Of the five senses two are uninjured.
   Used with reference to some, of several desiderated objects that have
   been accomplished, or of some things that remain uninjured.
   77. añcu paṇam koṭuttālum, ittaṉai āttiram ākātu.
   Even if you were to give away five fanams, such haste or precipitation
   will not do.
   Precipitance in judgment is worse than actual loss.
   78. añcum mūṉṟum uṇṭāṉāl, aṟiyāp peṇṇum kaṟi camaikkum..
   If possessed of five and three, even a young girl may make a curry.
   The five are acid, pepper, salt, mustard and cumin; the three are
   water, fuel and fire.
   This proverb is used by a man when his wife has prepared a curry that
   does not please him.
   79. añcuruttāli neñcuruvak kaṭṭikkoṇṭu vantāṟpōl valakkāramāyp
   pēcukiṟāy.
   Thou speakest ostentatiously as a matron who has come having her bosom
   adorned with a tali ornamented with five figures.
   The tali is a marriage symbol used as a wedding ring is in Europe.
   Various astrological observances attend the melting of the gold &c. The
   five forms referred to are the five weapons of Vishnu. Viz. the dischs,
   the club, the conch, the bow and the sword. The Romanists ornament the
   tali with the figure of a dove.
   80. añcu vayatu āṇpiḷḷaikku aimpatu vayatup peṇ kāl muṭakkavēṇṭum.
   A woman of fifty must bend the knee before a boy of five.
   Referring to the deference paid to the male sex by the Hindus.
   81. añcuvōraik keñcaṭikkap pārkkiṟāṉ.
   He aims to humble the timid by force.
   82. añcūrccaṇṭai cuṇṭāṅki, aiṅkala arici oru kavaḷam.
   Conflict in five villages is but curry, five maracals of rice is only a
   mouthful.
   Spoken of one who delights in the misfortunes of others.
   83. añceḻuttum pāvaṉaiyum avaṉaippōl irukkiṟatu.
   The five letters and the sentiment are like him.
   Spoken of a child who in feature and disposition resembles its father.
   84. aṭakkattup peṇṇukku aḻakēṉ?
   Of what use is beauty to a damsel living in retirement?
   85. aṭakkamuṭaiyār aṟiñar, aṭaṅkātār kallār.
   The well-ordered are wise; the disorderly are fools.
   86. aṭakkamē peṇṇukku aḻaku.
   Modesty is the ornament of a woman.
   The proverb may be used of moderation generally and self-restraint.
   87. aṭakkuvāraṟṟa kaḻukkāṇiyum koṭṭuvāraṟṟa mēḷamumāyt
   tirikiṟāṉ.
   Adwarf without restraint—an unused tomtom-- he wanders about.
   Used of one irrecoverably worthless.
   88. aṭaṅkāta pāmpiṟku rājā mūṅkilttaṭi.
   A bamboo staff is the king of a vicious snake.
   89. aṭaṅkāta peṇcātiyiṉālē attaikkum namakkum pollāppu.
   A disobedient wife is an evil both to her mother and to one's self.
   Spoken of a person inimical alike to two parties.
   90. aṭaṅkāta maṉaiviyum āṅkāra puruṣaṉum.
   A disobedient wife and a self-willed husband.
   Said of uncongenial society.
   91. aṭaṅkiṉa piṭi piṭikkavēṇṭum, aṭaṅkāta piṭi piṭikkalākātu.
   Seize that which can be grasped, not that which cannot.
   Aim at the practicable.
   92. aṭampaṅkoṭiyum tiraṇṭāl miṭukku.
   Even tender creepers when united are strong.
   93. aṭā eṉpāṉ, veḷiyē puṟappaṭāṉ.
   He will speak abusively, but will not come out.
   94. aṭātu ceytavar paṭātu paṭuvār.
   They who do what they ought not, will suffer what they might have
   avoided.
   It serves him right.
   95. aṭi atiracam kuttuk koḻukkaṭṭai
   A slap is a cake, a cuff is sweetmeat.
   Spoken of one who is beyond ordinary discipline.
   96. aṭi utavukiṟatu pōla aṇṇaṉ tampi utavuvārkaḷā?
   Will an elder or younger brother aid one as effectually as discipline
   or punishment ?
   97. aṭikka aṭikkap pantu vicai koḷḷum.
   The more a ball is struck, the more it rebounds.
   98. aṭikkiṟa kāṟṟukkum peykiṟa maḻaikkum payappaṭa vēṇṭum.
   Beware of a beating wind and of falling rain.
   Beware of things beyond human control.
   99. aṭikkiṟa kāṟṟu veyilukkup payappaṭumā?
   Does the beating wind fear the sunshine ?
   Spoken of irrelevant means to subdue an evil.
   100. aṭikkum kāṟṟilē eṭuttut tūṟṟavēṇṭum.
   Winnow while the wind blows.
   101. aṭikkum oru kai, aṇaikkum oru kai.
   One hand smites, the other embraces.
   Discipline regulated by love; used sometimes of Divine chastisements.
   102. aṭiccaṭṭikkuḷḷē karaṇam pōṭalāmā?
   Can one make a somersault in the bottom of a chatti?
   103. aṭitta ērukkum kuṭitta kūḻukkum cari.
   What he received for his labour (ploughing) only paid for the kanji he
   drank.
   104. aṭittup paḻuttatum paḻamā?
   Is that which ripens by force fruit?
   (A favour done under constraint is no favour.)
   105. aṭittup pāl pukaṭṭukiṟatā?
   Is a child to be beaten in order to pour milk into its mouth?
   106. aṭittu vaḷarkkāta piḷḷaiyum muṟukki vaḷarkkāta mīcaiyum
   cevvaiyākā .
   Neither the child that is unchastised, nor the mustache that is
   untwirled will be right.
   Implying that due care must be exercised to secure a desiderated
   result.
   107. aṭittuviṭṭavaṉ piṉṉē pōṉālum, piṭittu viṭṭavaṉ piṉṉē pōkalākātu.
   Though you may trust one who has administered correction, you may not
   trust one who has betrayed you.
   108. aṭi nākkilē nañcum nuṉi nākkilē amirtamumā ?
   What! is it nectar at the tip of the tongue while poison is at the
   root?
   Said of one, or to one whose professions are at variance with his
   intentions.
   109. aṭi nocci nuṉi āmaṇakkā?
   Is the stem nochchi, and the top a castor plant?
   Intimating that the same characteristics will prevail in a family or
   tribe.
   110. aṭippāṉēṉ piṭippāṉēṉ? aṭakkukiṟa vaḻiyil aṭakkuvōm.
   Why beat him, why seize him? we will subdue him by proper means.
   111. aṭimēl aṭi aṭittāl, ammiyum nakarum.
   Even a grindstone will move under repeated strokes.
   112. aṭimai paṭaittāl āḷvatu kaṭaṉ.
   It is the duty of one who has a slave to employ him.
   113. aṭiyaṟṟa paṉai pōl viḻuntāṉ.
   He fell as a palmira tree severed from its root.
   So perfect was his prostration. This may be used of obeisance or ruin.
   114. aṭiyaṟṟa maram pōlē.
   As a tree without root.
   115. aṭiyaṟṟāl nuṉi viḻāmalirukkumā?
   If the root fail, will not the top fall?
   116. aṭiyā nāṭu paṭiyātu.
   A province in which authority is not enforced will not submit.
   117. aṭiyāta māṭu paṭiyātu.
   An unchastised bullock will not obey.
   118. aṭiyiluḷḷatu naṭuvukkum muṭivukkum uṇṭu.
   That which exists in the bottom is likewise in the middle and top.
   119. aṭiyum paṭṭup puḷitta māṅkāyā tiṉṉavēṇṭum?
   And besides being beaten, must (one) also eat sour mangoes?
   Spoken of something imposed on one who is already suffering from
   questionable inflictions.
   120. aṭiyeṉṟu aḻaikkap peṇṭīr illai , āṇ ettaṉai peṇ
   ettaṉai eṉkiṟatu pōla.
   Like saying to a man how many boys have you, and how many girls, when
   he has not a wife to whom he can say, Adi.
   121. aṭivāṉam kaṟuttāl, āṇṭai vīṭu vaṟukkum.
   If the horizon becomes black, the household of the farmer will begin to
   parch.
   122. aṭukkaḷaik kiṇaṟṟilē amutam eḻuntāṟpōla.
   Like the springing up of nectar ip a kitchen well.
   123. aṭukkaḷaikku oru peṇṇum ampalattukku oru āṇum
   irukkiṟateṉkiṟāṉ.
   He says, to cook, there is a woman; for outdoor work, a man.
   Spoken of needful help already at hand.
   124. aṭukkukiṟa arumai teriyumā uṭaikkiṟa nāykku?
   Does the dog that breaks (the cooking pots) know how difficult it is to
   arrange them?
   Used when a heedless clumsy person has disarranged or spoiled some
   cherished work on which great pains had been bestowed.
   125. aṭutta kūrai vēvuṅkāl taṉ kūraikku mōcam.
   When a neighbour's roof is in flames one's own is in danger.
   126. aṭuttavaṉaik keṭukkalāmā?
   Is it right to destroy one's neighbour?
   127. aṭuttavaṉ vāḻap pakalē kuṭi eṭuppāṉ.
   Because his neighbour prospers he removes in the day-time i. e., at
   once.
   128. aṭuttāraik keṭuttu aṉṉam iṭṭāraik kaṉṉam iṭukiṟāṉ.
   He destroys those about him and robs those who fed him.
   129. aṭuttu aṭuttuc coṉṉāl toṭutta kāriyam muṭiyum.
   By continually urging, the work undertaken may be completed.
   130. aṭuttuk keṭuppāṉ kapaṭaṉ, toṭuttuk koṭuppāḷ vēci.
   A deceiver destroys when near, a harlot in contact.
   131. aṭuttu vantavarkku ātaravu colluvōṉ kuru.
   He is a teacher or spiritual guide who gives wholesome counsel to those
   who resort to him.
   132. aṭupparukil veṇṇeyai vaitta katai.
   A story about butter being placed near the hearth.
   133. aṭuppuk kaṭṭikku aḻaku vēṇṭumā?
   Is ornamentation requisite in a hearth or fireplace? The needful is
   sufficient in common things.
   134. aṭuppu neruppum pōy vāyttaviṭum pōccutu.
   No fire in the hearth, no bran in the mouth.
   135. aṭuppu erintāl pori poriyum.
   Should the fire burn, you may parch grain.
   136. aṭaintōrai ātari.
   Take care of those who are under your protection.
   137. aṭaippaip piṭuṅkiṉāl pāmpu kaṭikkum.
   A snake will bite him who removes a fence.
   138. aṭaimaḻaikkuḷḷē ōr āṭṭukkuṭṭi cettatupōla.
   As a lamb died in heavy rain.
   Said of one coming to grief from want of due care.
   139. aṭaimaḻai viṭṭum ceṭimaḻai viṭavillai.
   Though the heavy rain is over, the dropping from the trees continues.
   Though the greater evils have passed away, the lesser remain. Though
   the spoiler is not upon us, the ordinary imposts are demanded by men in
   power
   140. aṭṭamattuc caṉi kiṭṭa vantatupōla .
   As Saturn in the eighth sign approached.
   Some person or thing ominous of evil.
   141. aṭṭamattuc caṉi naṭṭam varac ceyyum.
   Saturn in the eighth sign will bring loss.
   See the preceeding.
   142. aṭṭamattuc caṉi piṭittatu, arait tuṇiyum urintu koṇṭatu.
   Saturn in the eighth sign seized, and stripped off even the waist
   cloth.
   143. aṭṭamattuc caṉiyai vaṭṭikku vāṅkiṉāṟpōla.
   Like borrowing Saturn in the eighth sign on interest.
   144. aṭṭaikkum tiruttiyillai , akkiṉikkum tiruttiyillai.
   The leech is not satisfied, nor is fire. Inordinate desire is never
   satisfied.
   145. aṭṭaiyaip piṭittu mettaiyil vaittatu pōla.
   As a reptile caught and placed on a cushion.
   Said of a person in an unnatural situation.
   146. aṭṭaiyai eṭuttu mettaiyil vaittālum cettaiyaiyē nāṭum.
   Although you take a reptile and place it on a cushion, it will seek a
   heap of dried leaves.
   147. aṇil ēṟaviṭṭa nāy pārppatupōla.
   As a dog looks up at a squirrel that has ascended and escaped his
   grasp.
   A despairing look after a thing irrecoverably lost.
   148. aṇiṟpiḷḷaikku nuṅku aritō? āṇṭicci piḷḷaikkuc
   cōṟu aritō?
   Does the squirrel lack the pulp of the young palmyra fruit, or the
   child of a Saiva mendicant, rice ?
   To give to religious mendicants is held, by the Hindus generally, to be
   meritorious.
   149. aṇu makāmēruvāmā?
   Is an atom the great Meru?
   The sacred mountain Meru, forming the centre of the seven continents
   according to the Mythological geography of the Hindus ; it appears to
   mean the highland of Tartary north of the Himalaya.
   150. aṇuvum malaiyāccu, malaiyum aṇuvāccu.
   An atom is become a mountain, a mountain an atom.
   151. aṇai kaṭanta veḷḷam aḻutālum varumā?
   Though one cry after it, will the flood that has burst its bund return
   ?
   As a premonition, this proverb is used to inculcate caution, andas a
   remark on misfortune, it suggests the uselessness of regret.
   152. aṇai kaṭanta veḷḷattai maṟippavar ār?
   Who can stop a flood that has burst its bund?
   Spoken of insuperable evils.
   153. aṇṭattiṟku uḷḷatu piṇṭattiṟkum uṇṭu.
   Whatever exists in the universe exists in the human body.
   154. aṇṭattil illātatum piṇṭattil uṇṭā?
   Does that exist in the body which does not exist in the universe ?
   The greater includes the lesser. This and the preceeding proverb really
   relate to the dialectics of the learned and philosophic schools.
   155. aṇṭattaic cumakkiṟavaṉukkuc cuṇṭaikkāy pāramā?
   Will a fruit be burdensome to Him who bears the universe ?
   Used when some favour is sought of one who is known to possess ample
   resources.
   156. aṇṭamum piṇṭamum antaraṅkamum veḷiyaraṅkamum.
   Of the universe and the body, the former is hidden, the latter open.
   157. aṇṭāta piṭāri ārukku aṭaṅkuvāḷ?
   Whom will the unapproachable demoness obey?
   The term Pidari is generally applied to a village goddess who is
   regarded as an evil being.
   158. aṇṭai vīṭṭuc caṇṭai kaṇṇukkuk kuḷircci.
   A quarrel in a neighbouring house is refreshing.
   Spoken of envious persons who may be supposed to delight in a
   neighbour's shortcomings or misfortunes
   159. aṇṭai vīṭṭup pārppāṉ caṇṭai mūṭṭit tīrppāṉ.
   The neighbouring Brahman will breed a quarrel and settle it.
   Said of one who promotes evil in order that he may gain by it.
   160. aṇṭaiyiṟ camarttaṉ illāta rājāvukku apakīrtti varum.
   Disgrace will attach to a king who has not a competent person near him.
   161. aṇṇaṉār araṇmaṉaiyil aḷḷi uṇṇap pōkiṟāḷ.
   She is going to eat rice in her elder brother's house.
   162. aṇṇaṉiṭattil āṟu mātam vāḻntālum, aṇṇiyiṭattil arai nāḻikai
   vāḻalāmā?
   Although one may live six months with an elder brother, one cannot
   abide with his wife even half an hour.
   The first condition is proverbially difficult, how much more so the
   second?
   163. aṇṇaṉukkup peṇ piṟantāl, attai acal nāṭṭāḷ.
   If my elder brother has a daughter, my paternal aunt becomes an alien.
   164. aṇṇa ṉum tampiyum ceṉmap pakaivar.
   An elder and younger brother are natural (birth) enemies.
   They are supposed to be subject to envy, jealousy and hatred on account
   of the rights they inherit.
   165. aṇṇaṉaik koṉṟa paḻi, cantaiyilē tīrttukkoḷḷukiṟatu pōla.
   Like taking revenge in the market-place, or in the place where the
   Vedas are read, on account of an elder brother who has been murdered.
   166. aṇṇaṉ periyavaṉ, appā neruppu ūtu.
   My elder brother is superior, father, blow the fire.
   Said when an inferior is treated with grouter deference than one really
   entitled to distinction.
   167. aṇṇaṉ kompu pampaḻa paḷāccu.
   The horn of my elder brother is as soft (harmless) as a shivered stick.
   The foxes of a certain region familiar with the hare had, for a long
   time, an idea that its fine long ears were formidable horns, and there
   fore kept aloof from it. At length an old fox proposed that they should
   try to ascertain the nature of the extraordinary excrescences on its
   head; and suggested that some of their neighbours should be invited to
   a feast. This was done ; and when all things were ready the foxes led
   their guests into the festive hall and they were seated a fox and a
   hare alternately, at table. During the feast one of the foxes
   professing great admiration for the fine horns of their guests, laid
   his hand upon one and looking round uttered the Proverb. On
   hearing this, each for seized and devoured the hare next to him.
   168. aṇṇaṉ uṇṇātatu ellām maittuṉikku lāpam.
   Whatever is left uneaten by my elder brother is an advantage to my
   sister-in-law.
   169. aṇṇaṉ tampi vēṇṭum iṉṉam tampirāṉē.
   Elder brother and younger brother are still wanted, O God.
   170. aṇṇāviyār viḻuntāl aṭaivumuṟai.
   Should a teacher make a slip, it is attributed to his art.
   171. aṇṇāvi piḷḷaikkup paṇam pañcamā , ampaṭṭaṉ piḷḷaikku mayirpañcamā?
   Is the child of an artist (a dancing-master) without money, is the
   son of a barber in want of hair ?
   172. aṇṇākkum toṇṭaiyum atira aṭaittatu.
   It obstructed the throat and the uvula so as to cause them to quake.
   173. aṇṇāmalaiyār aruḷuṇṭāṉāl maṉṉārcāmi mayiraip piṭuṅkumā?
   If the favour of Annamalai (Siva) be obtained, will the god Mannnar
   pull out a hair?
   Safe as regards an inferior, if protected by a superior.
   174. aṇṇāmalaiyārukku aṟupattu nālu pūcai, āṇṭikaḷukku eḻupattu nālu
   pūcai .
   The forms of worship prescribed for Siva are sixty-four; whereas the
   seasons for feeding religious mendicants are seventy-four.
   175. atika ācai atika naṣṭam.
   Excessive desire entails excessive loss.
   176. atika ācai aṣṭa tarittiram.
   Excessive desire or greed ends in eight forms of poverty,
   The eight forms of poverty are the absence or negation of the eight
   sources of fruition or enjoyment. These are peṇ women ; āṭai apparel ;
   aṇikalaṉ jewels ; pōcaṉam food ; tāmpūlam betel ; parimaḷam perfumes ;
   pāṭṭu Songs ; pūvamaḷi reclining on flowers.
   177. atikāriyum talaiyāriyum oṉṟāṉāl, viṭiyumaṭṭum tiruṭalām.
   If the chief and headman are united, stealing may be carried on till
   day - break.
   178. atikāri vīṭṭil tiruṭit talaiyāri vīṭṭil vaittatu pōla.
   As one placed in the house of the headman what he had stolen in the
   house of the chief.
   Said of a thing done unwittingly.
   179. atikāri vīṭṭuk kōḻi muṭṭai kuṭiyāṉavaṉ vīṭṭu ammiyai uṭaikkum.
   An egg obtained from the house of the chief will break the grindstone
   in the house of the peasant.
   Implying that authority imparts to the insignificant overwhelming
   power.
   180. atiṣṭam toṭṭa kaḻukkāṇi.
   A short thick person favoured by fortune.
   181. atiṣṭam āṟāyp perukukiṟatu.
   Fortune flows as a river.
   182. atiṣṭam iruntāl aracu paṇṇalām.
   When fortune smiles one may reign as a king.
   183. atiṣṭam illātavaṉukkuk kalappāl vantālum, ataiyum pūṉai kuṭikkum.
   Although the unfortunate gets a large measure of milk, the cat will lap
   it all up.
   Destiny prevails to thwart benefits when one is unfortunate.
   184. atiṣṭavāṉ maṇṇait toṭṭālum poṉṉākum.
   When touched by the fortunate even earth becomes gold.
   185. atira aṭittāl, utira viḷaiyum.
   If thoroughly ploughed, the yield will be abundant.
   186. atilum itu putumai , itilum atu putumai.
   This is stranger than that, and that is stranger than this.
   187. atilē kuṟaiccal illai āṭṭaṭā pūcāri.
   O, priest there is no lack, proceed with the ceremony.
   Said to a Pujachari by way of encouragement.
   188. atu ataṟku oru kavalai, aiyāvukku eṭṭuk kavalai.
   Each object is attended by a single anxiety, whereas the master has
   eight.
   189. atu ellām uṇṭiṭṭu vā eṉpāṉ.
   He will eat it all and say come.
   190. atukku iṭṭa kācu miṉakkeṭṭu arivāḷmaṇaikkuc cuṟukkiṭṭatā?
   Did the money, diverted from the object for which it was given, avail
   for the purchase of a sickle ?
   191. atuvum pōtāteṉṟu aḻalāmā iṉi?
   May you hereafter weep because that also is too little ?
   192. atairiyamuḷḷavaṉai añcāta vīraṉ eṉṟāṟpōla.
   Like calling a coward a fearless hero'
   Said of false adulation.
   193. atai viṭṭālum katiyillai, appuṟam pōṉālum vaḻiyillai.
   If that is renounced there is no help, if you go further there is no
   way.
   Spoken of evils from which there is no escape.
   194. attam mikutiyiṉāl allavō ampaṭṭaṉ peṇ kēṭkiṟāṉ?
   It is by reason of his great wealth, is it not, that the barber asks a
   maiden in marriage ?
   195. attaṉaiyum nērntāḷ, uppiṭa maṟantāḷ.
   Although she put in so many ingredients, she omitted salt.
   Said of something complete in all but one essential.
   196. attāṉ cettāṉ mayirāccu, kampaḷi mettai namakkāccu.
   My brother-in-law's death has not affected me any more than the loss of
   a hair; but his blanket and mattress have become mine.
   197. attikkāyaip piṭṭuppārttāl, aṅkum iṅkum poḷḷal.
   If you break open a fig, you will see cavities here and there.
   198. atti pūttatupōl irukkiṟatu avaṉ vantatu.
   His coming is like the flowering of the fig tree.
   Something that never happens.
   199. attip paḻattaip piṭṭuppārttāl, attaṉaiyum puḻuttāṉ.
   The more a fig is opened, the greater will be the number of worms
   found.
   200. attippūvai ār aṟivārkaḷ?
   Who ever saw the flower of a fig-tree?
   201. attippūvaik kaṇṭavarkaḷ uṇṭā? āntaik kuñcaip pārttavarkaḷ uṇṭā ?
   Are there any who have seen the blossom of the fig-tree ? are there any
   who have seen the young of an owl?
   Said of things that rarely or never happen.
   202. attimarattil tottiya kaṉipōla.
   Like fruit sticking to a fig-tree.
   203. attu mīṟippōṉāṉ, pittukkoḷḷi āṉāṉ.
   He transgressed and became mad.
   204. attaikku mīcai muḷaittāṟ ciṟṟappā eṉkalām.
   Should the mustache of one's aunt grow we may call her uncle.
   Referring to improbable contingencies.
   205. antam cintiṉavaṉukku aḻaku oḻukumā ?
   Will beauty emanate from one void of symmetry ?
   206. antaṇarkkut tuṇai vētam.
   The Veda is the staff of the Brahman.
   207. antaṇar maṉaiyiṟ cantaṉam maṇakkum.
   The house of the Brahman is perfumed.
   It being a requisite for ceremonial usages.
   208. antarattil kōl eṟinta antakaṉ pōla.
   Like a blind man who has thrown his staff into the air.
   209. anti īcal pūttāl, aṭaimaḻai atikarikkum.
   If white-ants take wing in the evening, it prognosticates excessive
   rain.
   210. anti maḻai aḻutālum viṭātu.
   The evening rain will not cease even if one should weep.
   211. antūtu nellāṉēṉ.
   I am become as useless as a grain of paddy eaten by insects.
   212. appacci kutampaiyaic cūppap piḷḷai muṟṟiṉa tēṅkāykku aḻukiṟatu
   pōla.
   While the father is sucking the husk of the cocoanut his son is crying
   for the kernel or pulp.
   Indicating desires beyond one's means.
   213. appacci kōvaṇattaip paruntu koṇṭōṭukiṟatu, piḷḷai vīravāḷip
   paṭṭukku aḻukiṟatu.
   While the hawk snatches away the father's waist-cloth his daughter is
   crying for a silk dress.
   Said in reproof when wishes are entertained beyond one's means.
   214. appattai eppaṭic cuṭṭīrkaḷ? tittippai eppaṭi nuḻaittīrkaḷ?
   How did you bake the cake ? how did you sweeten it ?
   215. appam cuṭṭatu caṭṭiyil, aval iṭittatu tiṭṭaiyil,
   Cakes are baked in a chatty, steeped rice is flattened in a mortar.
   Means must be suited to the end.
   216. appam eṉṟāṟ piṭṭukkāṭṭa vēṇṭumā?
   If bread, is it necessary to break and prove it ?
   217. appaṉ arumai appaṉ māṇṭāl teriyum, uppiṉ arumai uppuc camaintāl
   teriyum.
   The value of a father is known after his decease, that of salt when
   exhausted.
   218. appaṉ cōṟṟukku aḻukiṟāṉ, piḷḷai kumpakōṇattil kōtāṉam ceykiṟāṉ.
   The father is crying for rice and his son is performing the ceremony of
   giving a cow at Kumbhakónam.
   219. appaṉ periyavaṉ, ciṟṟappā curuṭṭukku neruppuk koṇṭuvā eṉkiṟatu
   pōla.
   Like saying, my father is a great person, uncle, bring fire to light my
   cigar.
   Pride of wealth leads one to neglect the observance of established
   social rules.
   220. appā eṉṟāl ucci kuḷirumā ?
   If one exclaim, alas ! alas! will the crown of the head become cool ?
   Expressions of sympathy are unavailing if not associated with real
   help.
   221. appiyācam kūcāvittai .
   Experience is knowledge that maketh not ashamed.
   222. appiyāca vittaikku aḻivu illai.
   Thoroughly acquired knowledge does not fail.
   Said in commendation of obvious efficiency.
   223. amañci uṇṭō kuppunāykkarē eṉpāṉēṉ?
   Why ask of the military officer if there is any compulsory service ?
   Why gratuitously seek avoidable evil?
   224. amarapaṭcam pūrvapaṭcam, kiṣṇapaṭcam cukkila paṭcam.
   The latter and former halves of the moon—its dark and bright sides.
   'These symbolize the light and darkness, the gladness and sadness of
   human life.
   225. amarikkai āyiram peṟum.
   Quietness is worth thousands of gold.
   226. amarttaṉukkum kāṇi vēṇṭām, camarttaṉukkum kāṇi vēṇṭām.
   An oppressor and a clever man need no landed property.
   227. amāvācaik karukkalilē peruccāḷi pōṉatellām vaḻi.
   In the darkness of the new moon the bandycoote finds a way wherever he
   goes.
   228. amāvācaip parukkai eṉṟaikkum akappaṭumā?
   Is the food peculiar to the new moon to be had every day ?
   229. amutam uṇkiṟa vāyāl viṣam uṇpārkaḷā?
   Will poison be received by the mouth accustomed to nectar ?
   230. ampaṭṭakkirutum vaṇṇāra oyilum.
   The arrogance of a barber and the affectation of a washerman.
   Said when inferiors give themselves airs of importance.
   231. ampaṭṭaṉ kuppaiyaik kiḷaṟiṉāl mayir mayirāyp puṟappaṭum.
   If the rubbish heap of the barber be stirred, nothing but hair turns
   up.
   The more you examine an inferior thing, the viler will it appear.
   232. ampaṭṭaṉ piḷḷaikku mayir arukkāṇiyā?
   Is hair a rare sight to the child of a barber ?
   233. ampalak kaḻutai ampalattiṟ kiṭantāl eṉṉa, aṭutta tirumāḷikaiyiṟ
   kiṭantāl eṉṉa ?
   What matters it whether the helpless ass lies in an open place or in
   the adjoining palace.
   234. ampalattil ēṟum pēccai amacaṭakkam paṇṇap pārkkiṟāṉ.
   He is aiming to conceal the rumour which is to be brought before the
   public assembly (for discussion.)
   235. ampalattil kaṭṭuccōṟu aviḻkkiṟāppōl.
   As boiled rice tied up for a journey is untied in an open place.
   236. ampalattil poti aviḻkkal ākātu.
   It is not good to unpack in an open place.
   Unnecessary exposure of one's personal affairs is to be avoided.
   237. ampāṇi taittatu pōlap pēcukiṟāṉ.
   He speaks like piercing arrows.
   238. ampikoṇṭu āṟu kaṭappōr, nampikkoṇṭu narivāl koḷḷuvārkaḷā?
   Will those who cross the river on a raft entrust themselves to the tail
   of a jackal ?
   239, ammaṇamum iṉṉalum āyucupariyantamā?
   Are nakedness and misery to continue to the end of life ?
   240. ammā keṭṭa kēṭṭukku mukkāṭu oṉṟā?
   Is a veil necessary for a woman in a bad condition ?
   241. ammāṉum marumakaṉum oru vīṭṭukku āḷ aṭimai.
   The father-in-law and son-in-law are slaves in the same house.
   242. ammāṉ makaḷukku muṟaiyā?
   Why enquire after the relationship of the daughter of one's maternal
   uncle ?
   243. ammāṉ vīṭṭu veḷḷāṭṭiyai aṭikka atikāriyaik kēṭka vēṇṭumā?
   Having to chastise a maid-servant in a father-in-law's house, is it
   necessary to ask the chief permission to do so ?
   244, ammi miṭukkō araippavaḷ miṭukkō?
   Whether is stronger, the grindstone or she who grinds ?
   245. ammi mitittu aruntati pārttatu pōla.
   Like one standing on a grindstone and looking at Arundathi.
   Arundathi is a star in the Great Bear regarded as the wife de Vasishta,
   a pattern of chastity. This star is pointed out to the bride at the
   marriage ceremony.
   246. ammiyiruntu aracaṉai aḷippāḷ.
   She will bring forth a king on the grindstone.
   During the time of child-birth it is not uncommon for the mothers to be
   seated on a grindstone.
   247. ammiyum kuḻaviyum ākāyattil paṟakkaccē, ecciṟ kallai eṉakku eṉṉa
   putti eṉṟāṟ pōla.
   As a leaf-plate sought advice when the grindstone and its rollerwere
   flying in the air.
   218. ammukkaḷḷi āṭaiyait tiṉṟāl veṇṇey uṇṭā?
   If a thievish woman eat the cream will there be any butter ?
   249. ammaikku amarkkaḷam poṅkip paṭaiyuṅkaḷ.
   The strife of Ammai, (a village goddess) has begun, boil and present
   (rice.)
   250. ammaiyār eppoḻutu cāvāḷ? kampaḷi eppoḻutu namakku miccamākum?
   When will my mistress die ? when shall I get her blanket ?
   251. ammaiyār nūṟkiṟa nūlukkum, pēraṉ kaṭṭukiṟa araiñāṭkayiṟṟukkum
   cari.
   The yarn spun by the old dame will only just suffice to form
   waist-string for her grandson.
   252. ammaiyār peṟukiṟatu araikkācu , talai ciraikkiṟatu mukkāl kācu.
   Three fourths of a cash is demanded for shaving the head of an old
   woman worth only half a cash.
   253. ammaiyārkku eṉṉa tukkam, kantait tukkam.
   What sorrow has the old dame ? that of raggedness.
   254, ammaiyārē vārum, kiḻavaṉaik kaikkoḷḷum.
   Come, old lady, and receive the old man.
   255. ayalār uṭamaikkup pēyāyp paṟakkiṟāṉ.
   He flies at his neighbours' property as a demon,
   256. ayalār uṭaimaiyil antakaṉ pōl iru.
   Be blind as regards your neighbours' property.
   257. ayaṉ iṭṭa kaṇakku ārukkum tappātu.
   No one escapes the decree of Brahma.
   258.ayaṉ iṭṭa eḻuttil aṇuvaḷavum tappātu.
   The writing of Brahma will not fail in the least.
   259. ayiraiyum caṟṟē arukkumām piṭṭukkuḷ pōṭṭup picaṟāmal.
   Even the Airai (small as it is) will give itself airs if it be not
   mixed with the food.
   260. ayōkkiyar aḻaku aparañcic cimiḻil nañcu.
   Beauty in the unworthy is poison in a casket of fine gold.
   261. ayyaṉ aḷantapaṭi.
   As God measured.
   262. ayyāvaiyar kūḻukku appaiyaṅkār tātāvā?
   Is Appaiyangar the dispenser of Aiyavaiyar's gruel ?
   263. hara hara eṉpatu peritō, āṇṭikku iṭuvatu peritō?
   Whether is greater as regards merit, to say Hara, Hara, or to give to
   the mendicant?
   264. arakkaṉ āṇṭāl eṉṉa, maṉitaṉ āṇṭāl eṉṉa?
   What matters it whether a demon or a man rules?
   265. araṅkiṉṟi vaṭṭāṭalum, nūlaṟiviṉṟip pēcalum oṉṟu.
   To play draughts without a board and to speak without a knowledge of
   the shastras are alike.
   266. aracaṉ aḷaviṟku ēṟiṟṟu.
   It reached even the king.
   267. aracaṉ illāta nāṭu , puruṣaṉ illāta vīṭu.
   A country without a king, a family without a head.
   268. aracaṉ uṭaimaikku ākāyam cākṣi.
   The sky is the witness regarding the king's property.
   269. aracaṉ aṉṟu kollum, teyvam niṉṟu kollum.
   The king kills at once, God delays and kills.
   270. aracaṉ kalliṉ mēl kattari kāykkiṟatu eṉṟāl, kottu āyiram kulai
   āyiram eṉpārkaḷ.
   If it be reported that brinjals are growing on a stone in the king's
   courtyard they will say that the outcome is thousands of bunches and
   thousands of clusters.
   271. aracaṉ eppaṭiyō appaṭiyē kuṭikaḷ.
   As is the king such are his subjects.
   272. aracaṉ evvaḻi kuṭikaḷum avvaḻi.
   The people follow the lead of their rulers
   273. aracaṉ vaḻippaṭṭatu avaṉi.
   It is the earth i.e., its inhabitants that follows the king.
   274. aracaṉ vaḻippaṭātavaṉ illai.
   No one is exempt from duty to the king.
   275. aracaṉukku oru col aṭimaikkut talaiccumai.
   As regards the king it is a word, to the subject it is a burden.
   276. aracaṉukku valiyār añcuvatu eḷiyārkku aṉukūlamāyirukkiṟatu.
   The fear of the king among those in power operates auspeciously as
   regards the poor.
   277. aracaṉukkut tuṇai vayavāḷ.
   The help of a king is his mighty sword.
   278. aracaṉum aravum cari.
   A king and a snake are alike.
   279. aracaṉum aḻalum cari.
   A king and fire are alike.
   280. aracaṉum neruppum pāmpum cari.
   A king, fire. and snake are alike.
   281. aracaṉai nampip puruṣaṉaik kaiviṭṭatu pōla.
   Like losing a husband through trusting in a king.
   282. aracukku illaic ciṟumaiyum perumaiyum.
   A king observes not the distinction between little and great.
   283. aracuṭaiyāṉai ākāyam kākkum.
   The heavens guard the king.
   284. araṇṭavaṉ kaṇṇukku iruṇṭatu ellām pēy.
   To the eye of the timid every thing obscure is a demon.
   285. arattai aram aṟukkum, vayirattai vayiram aṟukkum.
   A file will cut a file, diamonds cut diamonds.
   286. arapik kutiraiyilum aiyampēṭṭait taṭṭuvāṇi nallatu.
   An Aiyampettah pony is preferable to an Arab steed.
   287. aravattaik kaṇṭāl kīri viṭumā?
   If a mungoose see a snake will it let it escape?
   288. aravukku illaic ciṟumaiyum perumaiyum.
   The distinction of big and little does not apply to snakes.
   289. araṉ aruḷillāmal aṇuvum acaiyātu.
   Not an atom will move without the permission of God.
   290, araṉ aruḷ aṟṟāl, aṉaivarum aṟṟār.
   Should the favour of God fail, every one will come to nought.
   291. ari ari eṉṟāl, rāmā rāmā eṉkiṟāṉ.
   If I say Ari, Ari, he cries Rama, Rama.
   292. ari eṉkiṟa akṣaram terintāl atikāram paṇṇalām.
   If one understand the import of the word Ari, he may exercise regal
   power.
   293. ari eṉkiṟa akṣaram terintāl atikkiramam paṇṇalāmā?
   May one transgress the bounds of propriety if he comprehends the
   purport of the syllable Ari?
   294. ari eṉṟāl āṇṭikkuk kōpam, ara eṉṟāl tātaṉukkuk
   kōpam.
   If one says Ari the Saiva mendicant is angry, if one says Ara the
   Vaishnava mendicant is angry.
   295. arici aḷḷiṉa kākkāypōla.
   Like a crow that has taken np rice.
   296. arici āḻākkāṉālum, aṭuppukkaṭṭi mūṉṟu vēṇṭum.
   Though there be only one álak of rice, three supporting points in the
   hearth are required.
   Alak - a small measure of capacity.
   297. arici uṇṭāṉāl varicaiyum uṇṭu, akkāḷ uṇṭāṉāl
   maccāṉum uṇṭu.
   If there be rice there will be order—honour also, if a sister exist a
   brother-in-law may.
   298. arici uḻakkāṉālum aṭuppu mūṉṟu.
   Though there be only one ulak of rice, three supporting points in the
   hearth are required.
   An ulak is two alaks or the fourth of a measure.
   299. arici eṉṟu aḷḷip pārppārum illai, umi eṉṟu ūtippārppārum illai.
   No one takes up rice to examine it, nor does any one blow upon chaff.
   300. arici koṇṭu uṇṇa akkāḷ vīṭṭukkup pōvāṉēṉ?
   Seeing you have to pay for the rice you eat why go to the house of your
   elder sister?
   301. aricikkut takka ulaiyum āmuṭaiyāṉukkut takka vīṟāppum.
   Water in a pot proportioned to the rice, and a display of authority in
   a husband suited to his condition.
   302. aricip pakaiyum āmuṭaiyāṉ pakaiyum uṇṭā ?
   Does hatred against rice or a husband exist?
   303. aricip potiyuṭaṉ tiruvārūr.
   Tiruvaroor with a bag of rice.
   Spoken of a double benefit--the grain is valuable, and a visit to a
   sacred place is auspicious.
   304. arici maṟanta ulaikku uppu eṉṉa?
   Of what use is salt in a pot of boiling water without rice ?
   305. ariccantiraṉ vīṭṭukku aṭutta vīṭu.
   His house is next to Harischandra's.
   Used ironically of a notorious liar. Harischandra, a king famed for
   veracity.
   306. aritu aritu añceḻuttu uṇartal.
   It is difficult indeed to comprehend the purport of the five letter,
   The famous fire lettered incantation called the mūlamantiram asbeing
   the foundation of all spiritual wisdom and all religion, and the means
   prescribed and proper forobtaining emancipation from births and all the
   evils thereunto belonging, and union with the supreme.
   307. arittu erikkiṟa cuppikku āyam tīrvai uṇṭā ?
   Is tax levied op dry twigs scraped together for fuel ?
   308. ariya carīram antaratteṟinta kal.
   The precious body is a stone thrown into the air.
   309. ariyatu ceytu eḷiyatukku ēmāntu tirikiṟāṉ.
   Having performed difficult things he wanders about alarmed at that
   which is easy.
   310. ariyum civaṉum oṉṟu, alla eṉkiṟavaṉ vāyil maṇ.
   Vishnu and Siva are one; let sand be put into the mouth of him who
   denies it.
   311. arivāḷum acaiyavēṇṭum, āṇṭai kuṭiyum keṭavēṇṭum.
   The sickle must be moved, and the landlord's family must be ruined.
   312. arivāḷ cūṭṭaippōlak kāyccal māṟṟavō?
   Is it attempted to remove the heat of a burning fever as if it were
   that of a sickle ?
   313. arivāḷ cuṟukkē arivāḷmuṉai karukkē.
   Was it the dexterity of the reaper, or the sharpness of the sickle.
   Success in an enterprise is mainly owing to the means employed.
   314. arivai moḻi kēṭṭāl apattaṉ āvāṉ.
   He who listens to the words of a woman will be accounted worthless.
   315. arukākap paḻuttālum, viḷāmarattil vauvāl cērātu.
   Although the fruit of the wood-apple tree (Feronia elephantum) close by
   ripens, bats will not approach it.
   316. arukkāṇi nācciyār kuraṅkuppiḷḷaip peṟṟāvām.
   The haughty dame is said to have brought forth a monkey child.
   317. arukkāṇi muttuk karikkōlamāṉāḷ.
   She who was a precious pearl has become black as coal.
   318. arukkāmaṇiyā murukkam pūvā?
   Is it a rare gem or a Murukkam flower?
   319. aruṅkōṭait turumpu aṟṟuppōkutu.
   Through severe drought every blade has perished.
   320. aruṭcelvam ārukkum uṇṭu, poruṭcelvam ārukkum illai .
   Divine favour is common to all, material wealth is not.
   321. aruṇāmparamē karuṇāmparam.
   A cloth tinged red indicates the divine favour.
   Refers to the dress of religious mendicants.
   322. arumai aṟiyātavaṉ āṇṭu eṉṉa, māṇṭu eṉṉa?
   What matters it whether he who has no discrimination rules or dies ?
   323. arumai marumakaṉ talaipōṉālum pōkaṭṭum, ātikālattu ural pōkal
   ākātu.
   If it must be so, let the head of my son-in-law go; but the old
   mortar must not go.
   Said of things which long possession has made peculiarly precious.
   324. arumaiyaṟṟa vīṭṭil erumaiyum kuṭiyirātu.
   Even a buffalo will not abide in a house where it is not regarded.
   325. arum cuṉai nīr uṇṭāl, appoḻutē rōkam.
   The drinking of water from a hill reservoir will at once produce
   disease.
   326. aruḷ vēṇum, poruḷ vēṇum, aṭakkam vēṇum.
   Kindness, wealth and self-restraint are essentials.
   327. araikkācukku aḻinta kaṟpu āyiram poṉ koṭuttālum kiṭaiyātu.
   Chastity forfeited for half a cash, cannot be recovered though one
   should give thousands of gold.
   323. araikkācukkup pōṉa māṉam āyiram koṭuttālum vārātu.
   The honour that has been forfeited for half a cash, will not return
   though one give thousands.
   329. araikkiṉum cantaṉam ataṉ maṇam aṟātu.
   Though sandal wood bé rubbed, its fragrance will not be destroyed.
   330. araikkuṭam tatumpum niṟaikuṭam tatumpātu.
   The water in a pot half full wabbles, if full it wabbles not.
   331. araiccalliyai vaittu erukku ilaiyaik kaṭantatu pōla.
   As one leaped over an Erukku leaf, Calotropis gigantea, under which he
   hadconcealed half a challi.
   A cballi is a small copper coin, a fractional part of a larger coin.
   332. araiccīlai kaṭṭak kaikku upacāramā?
   Is the hand to be complimented for tying the waist-cloth?
   333. araiccoṟ koṇṭu ampalam ēṟalāmō?
   Can one go before an assembly with half a word?
   334. araiccoṟ koṇṭu ampalam ēṟiṉāl, araiccol muḻuc collākum.
   Half a word admitted into the assembly becomes a whole word.
   335. araitta payaṟu muḷaittāṟpōla.
   As if macerated gram should sprout.
   336. araittu mīntatu ammi ciraittu mīntatu kuṭumi.
   After grinding the stone remains; after shaving the head, the kudumi
   (tuft of hair) remains.
   A tuft of hair left on the head of a boy or man with a knot at the
   end. It is considered becoming ; and is usually about a foot in length.
   337. araippaṇam koṭuttu aḻac colli orupaṇam koṭuttu ōyaccolluvāṉēṉ?
   Having given half a fanam to weep, why give a fanam to cease ?
   Fanam - a small coin of gold or silver.
   338. araippaṇac cēvakam āṉālum araṇmaṉaic cēvakam pōl ākumā?
   Though the service bring half a fanam, will it be equal to service in
   the king's house?
   339. araippaṇam koṭukkap pālmāṟi, aimpatu poṉ koṭuttuc cērvai ceyta
   katai.
   A story of one who gave fifty gold pieces for a compound medicine after
   having hesitated to give half a fanam.
   340. araiyiṟ puṇṇum aṇṭai vīṭṭuk kaṭaṉum ākātu.
   Sores about the waist, and debt to one's neighbour are both bad.
   341. araivittai koṇṭu ampalam ēṟiṉāl araivittai muḻu vittai ākumā?
   If one goes before an assembly with half learning will it become
   perfect?
   342. aluttup piluttu akkāḷ vīṭṭukkup pōṉāl akkāḷ iḻuttumaccāṉiṭattiṟ
   pōṭṭāḷām.
   When he went to his elder sister's wearied and exhausted, it is said
   that she sent him to his brother-in-law.
   343 alai eppoḻutu oḻiyum, talai eppoḻutu muḻukukiṟatu ?
   When will the waves be still, when will he wash his head ?
   344. alai mōtum pōtē talai muḻukukiṟatu.
   Bathing in the midst of beating waves.
   Said of attempting to do a thing under adverse circumstances.
   345. alaivāyt turumpupōla.
   Like a straw on a wave.
   346. allakkāṭṭu nari pallaik kāṭṭukiṟatu pōla.
   Showing his teeth like a jackal of the ginger fields.
   Said of one who shows signs of disappointment,
   347. allavai tēya aṟam perukum.
   As vice wanes, virtue waxes.
   348. allaṟpaṭṭu aḻuta kaṇṇīr celvattait tēykkum paṭai.
   The bitter tears of the oppressed are a file that wears away the wealth
   (of the oppressor.)
   349. allal aṟṟa paṭukkai aḻakilum aḻaku.
   A bed free from anxiety is the most agreeable of all things.
   350. allal aruḷāḷvārkkillai.
   The kind-hearted are free from sorrow.
   351. allal oru kālam celvam orukālam.
   A time for adversity, and a time for prosperity.
   352. allātavaṉ vāyil kaḷḷai vār.
   Pour toddy into the mouth of the wicked.
   Add fuel to the file.
   353. allāta vaḻiyāl poruḷ īṭṭal kāmam tuyttal ivai ākā.
   Ill-gotten wealth and illicit pleasure are both bad.
   354. allum pakalum aḻukkaṟak kal.
   Study day and night to be free from impurity.
   355. avakaṭam uḷḷavaṉ arumai aṟiyāṉ.
   A wily man does not know the value (of friendship)
   356. avakuṇakkāraṉ ākācamāvāṉ.
   The vicious will evaporate into thin air.
   357. avacarakkāraṉukkup putti maṭṭu.
   The hasty are deficient in sense.
   358. avacarakkōlam aḷḷit teḷikkiṟēṉ.
   For hasty ornamentation I take up and sprinkle.
   359. avacarattil upacāramā?
   Is ceremonious behaviour demanded when one is in a hurry ?
   360. avacarattiṟkup pāvam illai.
   A thing done in an emergency is not criminal.
   361. avacaramāṉāl arikkaṉ caṭṭiyilum kai nuḻaiyātu.
   When in haste the hand will not enter even into a large chatty.
   362. avacāripōkavum ācai irukkiṟatu aṭippāṉ eṉṟu payamum irukkiṟatu.
   She is inclined to play the harlot, and is afraid that her husband will
   beat her.
   363. avacārikku vāy peritu, añcu āṟu aricikkuk koti peritu.
   The mouth of the harlot is large, and the bubbling of five or six
   grains of rice when boiling is excessive.
   361. avacārikku āṇai illai, tiruṭikkut teyvam illai.
   The harlot has no oath, the thief has no god.
   Spoken of one wholly derelict.
   365. avacāri āṭiṉālum atiṣṭam vēṇṭum tiruṭap pōṉālum tiṟamai vēṇṭum.
   If one plays the harlot fortune is wanted, and when one goes to steal
   ability is needed.
   366. avacāriyeṉṟu āṉaimēl ēṟalām tiruṭiyeṉṟu teruvil varalāmā?
   As an abandoned woman one may ride on an elephant, but can one go along
   a street as a thief?
   367. avatantiram taṉakku antaram.
   Chicanery ends in one's ruin.
   368. avatikkuṭikkut teyvamē tuṇai.
   God himself is the help of a family in extremities.
   369. avattaṉukkum camarttaṉukkum kāṇi kavai illai.
   The worthless and the cheat have no need of land.
   370. avappoḻutilum tavappoḻutu nallatu.
   Time is better spent in austerities than in vanity.
   371. avamāṉampaṇṇi vekumāṉam pēcukiṟāṉ.
   Having acted reproachfully he speaks flatteringly.
   372. avarā cuṟukkē arivāḷmaṇai karukkē.
   He is surely as sharp as the edge of a sickle.
   Spoken ironically of a man of vain pretensions.
   373. avaruṭaiya ciṟaku muṟintu pōyiṟṟu.
   His wing is broken.
   374. avar avar maṉacē avar avarkkuc cāṭci.
   Each one's mind is the witness that acquits or condemns.
   375. avar avar eṇṇattai āṇṭavar aṟivār.
   The supreme Ruler knows the purposes of every one.
   376. avalaṭcaṇam uḷḷa kutiraikkuc cuḻi cuttam pārkkiṟatillai.
   The circular curls of an ugly horse are not examined.
   377. avalamāy vāḻpavaṉ cavalamāyc cāvāṉ.
   He who leads a useless life will die a miserable death.
   378. avalai niṉaittu uralai iṭittāṟpōla.
   As if one beat an empty mortar thinking it contained unhusked: steeped
   rice.
   Spoken of absence of mind.
   379. avaḷ pāṭukiṟatu kuyil kūvukiṟatu pōl irukkiṟatu.
   Her singing is like the voice of the kuyil (an Indian cuckoo.)
   380. avaḷ camarttup pāṉai cantiyil uṭaintatu.
   Her best cooking-pot was broken in the middle of the road.
   381. avaṉukku ivaṉ eḻuntiruntu uṇpāṉ.
   This person will rise and eat before him.
   382. avaṉukkuk kappaṭāvum illai veṭṭukkattiyum illai.
   He has neither a cloth nor a knife.
   Said of ope utterly destitute.
   383. avaṉukkuḷḷē akappaṭṭirukkiṟatu eṉ piḻaippu.
   That which is in his keeping is my livelihood.
   384. avaṉukkuc cukkiraticai aṭikkiṟatu.
   He is now under the auspicious influence of the planet Venus.
   Spoken of one who is the favourite of fortune.
   385. avaṉukkum ivaṉukkum erumaiccaṅkāttam.
   The intimacy or friendship of these two persons is that of the buffalo.
   386. avaṉuṭaiya pēccu kāṟcollum araiccollum.
   His speech consists of quarters and halves of words.
   Undecisive.
   387. avaṉuṭaiya vāḻvu naṇṭukkuṭuvai uṭaintatu pōl irukkiṟatu.
   His prosperity is scattered like crabs from a broken vessel.
   388. avaṉē ivaṉē eṉpataiviṭac civaṉē civaṉē eṉkiṟatu nallatu.
   It is better to cry Siva, Siva, than opprobriously to reproach others.
   389. avaṉē veṭṭavum viṭavum karttaṉ.
   He has authority both to behead and to release.
   390. avaṉai avaṉ pēcciṭṭup pēccu vāṅki, āmai mallāttukiṟāppōl
   mallāttippōṭṭāṉ.
   Having fished out his secrets by cunning he turned him upside down as a
   turtle is turned.
   391. avaṉōṭu ivaṉai ēṇivaittup pārttālum kāṇātu.
   He cannot equal him though he raise himself to the top of a ladder.
   Said of one who is far inferior to another either socially or
   intellectually.
   392. avaṉ nā acaiya nāṭu acaiyum.
   When his tongue moves the country moves.
   393. avaṉ aruḷ aṟṟāl aṉaivarum aṟṟār , avaṉ aruḷ uṟṟālaṉaivarum uṟṟār.
   Without his favour all are destitute, if he favour, all are prosperous.
   Said of God's grace and displeasure.
   394. avaṉ oru kuḷirnta koḷḷi.
   He is a cold firebrand.
   Said of a person really dangerous though apparently not so.
   395. avaṉ mititta iṭattil pullum cākātu.
   Even the grass under his footsteps will not die.
   Used when speaking of one remarkable for gentle habits.
   396. avaṉ mititta iṭam paṟṟiyerikiṉṟatu.
   The place whereon he treads bursts into flames.
   Spoken of the reckless and violent.
   397. avaṉ metta attumiñciṉa pēccukkāraṉ.
   His words exceed all bounds.
   Said of one who delights in abusing others.
   398. avaṉ kālāl iṭṭa vēlaiyaik kaiyāl ceyvāṉ.
   The work that was indicated by the foot he will perform by the hand.
   399. avaṉ cāti entap putti kulam enta ācāramō atutāṉ varum.
   The devices of his caste and habits of his tribe will predominate.
   400. avaṉ kālāl muṭintataik kaiyāl aviḻkkakkūṭātu.
   That which he tied with his foot cannot be loosened by the hand.
   Spoken of one of superior skill.
   401. avaṉ acaiyātu aṇuvum acaiyātu.
   Unless he move not an atom will move.
   402. avaṉ avaṉ eṇṇattai āṇṭavaṉ ākkiṉālum ākkuvāṉ, aḻittālum aḻippāṉ.
   Every man's purpose will be either effectuated or frustrated by the
   Supreme Ruler.
   403. avaṉ kaḻuttukkuk katti tīṭṭukiṟāṉ.
   He is sharpening a knife for another's throat.
   404. avaṉ uḷḷellām puṇ uṭampellām koppuḷam.
   His mind is full of sores, his body is covered with blister.
   405. avaṉ eṉakku aṭṭamattuc caṉi.
   To me he is Saturn in the eighth sign.
   The most malignant of enemies.
   406. avaṉ totti uṟavāṭit tōlukku maṉṟāṭukiṟāṉ.
   He seeks friendship and prays for a skin.
   407. avaṉ taṉṉālē tāṉ keṭṭāl aṇṇāvi eṉṉa ceyvāṉ?
   What can his teacher do if he ruin himself?
   408. avaṉ kālāl kīṟiṉatai nāṉ nāvāl aḻikkiṟēṉ.
   What he has scored with his feet I efface with my tongue.
   409avaṉ uṉakkuk kiḷḷukkīraiyō?
   Do you consider him as a green-herb nipped off?
   410. avaṉ kaiyaik koṇṭē avaṉ kaṇṇil kuttiṉāṉ.
   He took another's hand and struck his eyes.
   411. avaṉ ciṟakillāp paṟavai.
   He is a bird without wings.
   412. avaṉ eṉ talaikku ulai vaikkiṟāṉ.
   He is preparing to cook my head.
   413. avaṉ avaṉ ceyta viṉai avaṉ avaṉukkē.
   Every one is responsible for his own actions.
   414. avaṉ eṉṉai ūtip paṟakkaṭikkap pārkkiṟāṉ.
   He aims to scatter me to the winds.
   415. avaṉ eriporiyeṉṟu viḻukiṟāṉ.
   He breaks out saying burn him, fry him.
   Said of one who suddenly bursts into a rage.
   416. avaṉ ōṭippāṭi nāṭiyil aṭaṅkiṉāṉ.
   Having run and sung, he at length sank like the pulse.
   417. avaṉ vallāḷakaṇṭaṉai vārippōriṭṭavaṉ.
   He is the man who encountered and fought the champion.
   418. avaṉ koñcappuḷḷiyā?
   Is he a man of limited wealth ?
   Spoken of a slovenly miser.
   419. avāntarattil akkiṉi paṟṟumā?
   Can fire be kindled in mid-air ?
   No, except by the Almighty "who holds the lightning in his hand and
   tells it whom to strike."
   420. avitta payaṟu muḷaitta katai pōla.
   Like the story of the germination of boiled peas.
   421. avivēki uṟavilum, vivēki pakaiyē naṉṟu.
   Better is the hatred of the wise than the friendship of fools.
   422. aviḻ eṉṉa ceyyum añcu kuṇam ceyyum poruḷ eṉṉa ceyyum pūvai vacam
   ceyyum.
   What can rice do? it can work the five senses, what can wealth do ? it
   can subdue the world.
   423. avaiyilum oruvaṉ cavaiyilum oruvaṉ.
   One who belongs to this convention and to that convention.
   424. avvaḷavu iruntāl aṭukkivaittu vāḻēṉō.
   If I had so much should I not arrange my chatties earthen cooking
   vessels and prosper.
   Spoken regretfully of succour which ought to be and is not afforded.
   425. aḻakilē piṟanta pavaḷakkoṭi akattilē piṟanta cāṇikkūṭai.
   In beauty equal to natural coral, at home a cow-dung basket.
   426. aḻakiṟku mūkkai aṟuppār uṇṭā?
   Are there any who cut the nose for the sake of beauty?
   427. aḻaku iruntu aḻum, atiṣṭam iruntu uṇṇum.
   Beauty will sit and weep, fortune will sit and eat.
   428. aḻakup peṇṇē kāṟṟāṭi uṉṉai aḻaikkiṟāṇṭi kūttāṭi.
   O beautiful maid, frail kite, the comedian calls you.
   Spoken of a female whoso demeanour is inconsistent with modesty.
   429. aḻakukku iṭṭāl āpattukku utavum.
   A jewel worn as an ornament may aid in adversity.
   Gold ornaments are often given as security for money borrowed to trade
   with, as well as to serve in pressing emergencies.
   430. aḻaccollukiṟavar tamar, cirikkaccollukiṟavar piṟar.
   They who advise weeping are one's friends, those who advise laughing
   are strangers.
   Spoken with reference to the character of advisers.
   431 aḻaccollukiṟavaṉ piḻaikkac colluvāṉ, cirikkac collukiṟavaṉ
   keṭaccolluvāṉ.
   He who counsels us to weep does so to favour life; he who counsels
   mirth seeks our ruin.
   432. aḻikkappaṭuvāṉaik kaṭavuḷ aṟivīṉaṉ ākkuvār.
   God dements him who is to be destroyed.
   433. aḻintavaḷ ārōṭu pōṉāl eṉṉa?
   What matters it with whom an abandoned woman goes ?
   434. aḻinta nantavaṉattil acuvam mēyntu eṉṉa, kaḻutai mēyntu eṉṉa?
   What matters it whether a horse or an ass grazes in a garden that lies
   waste ?
   435. aḻivaḻakkuc coṉṉavaṉ paḻi poṟukkum maṉṉavaṉ.
   One who seeks to maintain an unjust claim and a king who extenuates
   crime.
   Both are injurious to society.
   436. aḻukaḷḷar toḻukaḷḷar ācārakkaḷḷar.
   Weeping hypocrites, canting sycophants and religious formalists.
   437aḻukiṟa āṇaiyum cirikkiṟa peṇṇaiyum nampakkūṭātu.
   A weeping man and a smiling woman are not to be trusted.
   438. aḻukiṟa vīṭṭil iruntālum irukkalām, oḻukukiṟa vīṭṭil irukkalākātu.
   Though one may abide in a house of mourning, it is not possible to
   remain in one that is leaky.
   439. aḻukiṟa piḷḷaikku vāḻaippaḻam kāṭṭukiṟatu pōla.
   Like showing a ripe plantain to a crying child.
   A premium on naughtiness.
   440. aḻukiṟa vēḷai pārttu akkuḷ pāyccukiṟāṉ.
   He tickles when one is weeping.
   Spoken of something that is obtruded at an inconvenient time.
   441. aḻukiṟataṟku araip paṇam koṭuttu, ōykiṟataṟku oru paṇam koṭu.
   Having given half a fanam for weeping, give a fanam to cease.
   442. aḻukaiyum āṅkāramum cirippuk kelippōṭā?
   Are grief and self-will things to be made sport of ?
   443. aḻukkuc cīlaikkuḷḷē māṇikkam.
   A ruby in a dirty rag.
   444, aḻukkai aḻukkuk kollum iḻukkai iḻukkuk kollum.
   Dirt will remove dirt, reproach will overcome reproach.
   445. aḻukkait tuṭaittu maṭimēl vaittālum puḻukkaik kuṇam pōkātu.
   Though cleaned and placed on ope's knee, the mean disposition of a
   slave or low person will not leave him.
   446 aḻuta kaṇṇum cintiya mūkkum.
   A weeping eye and a running nose.
   447. aḻutavaṉukku akaṅkāramillai.
   He who is weeping has no pride.
   448. aḻutapiḷḷai pālkuṭikkum.
   A crying child obtains milk.
   449. aḻuta piḷḷai uram peṟum.
   The weeping child will gain strength.
   450. aḻutum piḷḷai avaḷē peṟa vēṇṭum.
   Although she may weep, she herself must bare the child.
   451. aḻutta neñcaṉ ārukkum utavāṉ, iḷakiṉa neñcaṉ evarkkum utavuvāṉ.
   The hard-hearted will be of use to none, the tender hearted will be of
   use to all.
   452. aḻuvār aṟṟa piṇamum āṟṟuvār aṟṟa cuṭalaiyum.
   A corpse unwept and a funeral pyre unquenched.
   An affair or person forsaken by all.
   453. aḻuvār aḻuvār tam tam tukkamē tiruvaṉ peṇṭīrukku aḻuvārillai.
   All weep on account of their own griefs, pone un account of Tiruvan's
   wife.
   Spoken of feigned sympathy and also of that outward expression of
   sorrow which is occasioned by surrounding circumstances.
   454. aḻaiyā vīṭṭiṟku nuḻaiyāc campanti.
   The mother of a bride or bridegroom will not visit their new relations
   unless invited.
   455. aḷakāpuriyilum viṟakutalaiyaṉ uṇṭu.
   There are firewood carriers even in Alagapuri, (the city of Kuvera.)
   Kuvera a wealthy king; now regent of the North and the guardian of
   riches.
   456. aḷakāpuri koḷḷaiyāṉālum, atiṣṭa īṉaṉukku oṉṟum illai.
   Although Alagapuri be given up to plunder, the unfortunate gains
   nothing thereby.
   457. aḷakēcaṉ āṉālum aḷavu aṟintu celavu ceyyavēṇṭum.
   Though as wealthy as Kuvera you must keep your expenses within due
   bounds.
   458. aḷakkiṟa nāḻi akavilai aṟiyumā?
   Does the measure know the price of grain ?
   459. aḷanta nāḻikoṇṭu aḷappāṉ.
   He will measure in the accustomed measure.
   460. aḷantāl oru cāṇillai arintāl oru caṭṭikāṇātu.
   When measured it is not a span long, when chopped up it does not fill a
   chatti.
   461. aḷantu aḷanta nāḻi oḻintu oḻintu varum.
   By repeated measurement articles will be diminished.
   Constant use wears out a thing.
   462. aḷavaḷāvillātavaṉ vāḻkkai kuḷavaḷāk kōṭiṉṟi nīr niṟaintaṟṟu.
   The prosperity of him who does not cultivate sociality is like the
   filling of a tank without a bank.
   463. aḷavukku miñciṉāl amirtamum nañcu.
